It's more that I feel completely exhausted, on edge, and overwhelmed by constant worry all the time. It really started wearing me down around the time my youngest niece left for college and I began seriously contemplating retirement amidst this unstable housing market.\n\nDoctor: Do you also get physical symptoms with the worry, such as restlessness, muscle tension, trouble sleeping, irritability, or feeling easily fatigued?"

My anxiety feels more like a constant, background worry and physical tension that just never goes away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden episodes of intense fear or panic that peak within minutes, with symptoms like shortness of breath, chest pain, dizziness, or feeling like something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those sudden, intense panic attacks with shortness of breath or dizziness. My anxiety is much more of a constant, lingering worry and physical tension that just never seems to lift.\n\nDoctor: How much has this constant worry and tension been affecting your daily life, such as your work, relationships, or ability to relax?"

It's more that I feel completely exhausted, on edge, and overwhelmed by constant worry all the time. That's actually why I've been turning down social invitations lately—I just don't have the energy to be present with people.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this constant worry and being on edge—has it been happening most days for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has been going on for well over six months—actually, for a little over a year now. It really started wearing me down around the time my youngest niece left for college and I began seriously contemplating retirement amidst this unstable housing market.\n\nDoctor: Do you also get physical symptoms with the worry, such as restlessness, muscle tension, trouble sleeping, irritability, or feeling easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes, my body is constantly tense—my shoulders and neck feel like concrete by the end of the day, and I clench my jaw so hard I get headaches. On top of that, I lie awake until 1 or 2 in the morning with my mind spinning, only to wake up at 4 AM with my heart racing, which leaves me feeling completely exhausted during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden episodes of intense fear or panic that peak within minutes, with symptoms like shortness of breath, chest pain, dizziness, or a feeling that something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have sudden panic attacks like that with chest pain or trouble breathing. My anxiety feels more like a constant, background worry and physical tension that just never goes away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden episodes of intense fear or panic that peak within minutes, with symptoms like shortness of breath, chest pain, dizziness, or feeling like something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those sudden, intense panic attacks with shortness of breath or dizziness. My anxiety is much more of a constant, lingering worry and physical tension that just never seems to lift.\n\nDoctor: How much has this constant worry and tension been affecting your daily life, such as your work, relationships, or ability to relax?\n\nPatient: It has had a major impact; I've started making mistakes at work, like missing clauses in contracts and forgetting to return client calls on time. I've also been turning down social invitations because I feel too exhausted and on edge to be present with friends or family. It has gotten to the point where I find it nearly impossible to relax at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you started, stopped, or changed any medications, caffeine, alcohol, or other substances around the time this anxiety began, or do you have any thyroid or other medical problems?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't started or changed any medications, and I don't drink much caffeine or alcohol. I also don't have any medical conditions like thyroid issues that I'm aware of."

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. It really started wearing me down around the time my youngest niece left for college and I began seriously contemplating retirement amidst this unstable housing market.\n\nDoctor: Do you also get physical symptoms with the worry, such as restlessness, muscle tension, trouble sleeping, irritability, or feeling easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes, my body is constantly tense—my shoulders and neck feel like concrete by the end of the day, and I clench my jaw so hard I get headaches. My anxiety feels more like a constant, background worry and physical tension that just never goes away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden episodes of intense fear or panic that peak within minutes, with symptoms like shortness of breath, chest pain, dizziness, or feeling like something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those sudden, intense panic attacks with shortness of breath or dizziness. My anxiety is much more of a constant, lingering worry and physical tension that just never seems to lift.\n\nDoctor: How much has this constant worry and tension been affecting your daily life, such as your work, relationships, or ability to relax?\n\nPatient: It has had a major impact; I've started making mistakes at work, like missing clauses in contracts and forgetting to return client calls on time. I've also been turning down social invitations because I feel too exhausted and on edge to be present with friends or family. It has gotten to the point where I find it nearly impossible to relax at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you started, stopped, or changed any medications, caffeine, alcohol, or other substances around the time this anxiety began, or do you have any thyroid or other medical problems?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't started or changed any medications, and I don't drink much caffeine or alcohol. I also don't have any medical conditions like thyroid issues that I'm aware of.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you find it very hard to control or stop the worry even when you know it may be excessive?\n\nPatient: Yes, it feels absolutely impossible to stop once it starts. Even when I tell myself, \"Parin, stop, this is ridiculous,\" the worries just chain one into another. If a deal falls through, I start spiraling about my commission, then my mortgage, and suddenly I'm picturing myself losing my home and having to move in with someone out of desperation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had brief, unexpected episodes of intense fear that peak within minutes, with symptoms like chest pain, shortness of breath, dizziness, or a feeling that something terrible is about to happen?"

from my retirement to whether my niece will be okay at college.\n- S057 (Uncontrollable_Worry): Even when I tell myself, ‘Parin, stop, this is ridiculous,’ the worries just keep chaining one into another—if a deal falls through I start thinking about my commission, then my mortgage, then my health insurance, and pretty soon I’m picturing myself having to sell my home and move in with someone out of desperation.\n- S059 (Concentration_Difficulty): These days I’ll be sitting in front of a purchase contract or an email from a lender and realize I’ve read the same paragraph three or four times because my mind keeps drifting off to what could go wrong with the deal or with my income, and sometimes, in the middle of explaining terms to a client, my mind just goes blank and I lose my train of thought mid‑sentence.\n- S061 (Muscle_Tension): By the end of most days my shoulders and neck feel like they’re made of concrete from how tightly I’m holding myself, and I catch myself clenching my jaw so hard during long drives between showings that I get headaches and my dentist has asked if I’m grinding my teeth at night.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- Lately I can’t seem to focus on my work the way I used to—my mind keeps drifting to everything that could go wrong, and I have to reread the same documents over and over. It has really started to concern me because I've always prided myself on being so sharp with my clients' contracts.\n\nDoctor: Besides the anxiety and trouble concentrating, have you also been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel down or hopeless, and I haven't lost interest in my life. It's more that I feel completely exhausted, on edge, and overwhelmed by constant worry all the time. That's actually why I've been turning down social invitations lately—I just don't have the energy to be present with people.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this constant worry and being on edge—has it been happening most days for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has been going on for well over six months—actually, for a little over a year now. It really started wearing me down around the time my youngest niece left for college and I began seriously contemplating retirement amidst this unstable housing market.\n\nDoctor: Do you also get physical symptoms with the worry, such as restlessness, muscle tension, trouble sleeping, irritability, or feeling easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes, my body is constantly tense—my shoulders and neck feel like concrete by the end of the day, and I clench my jaw so hard I get headaches. On top of that, I lie awake until 1 or 2 in the morning with my mind spinning, only to wake up at 4 AM with my heart racing, which leaves me feeling completely exhausted during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden episodes of intense fear or panic that peak within minutes, with symptoms like shortness of breath, chest pain, dizziness, or a feeling that something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have sudden panic attacks like that with chest pain or trouble breathing. My anxiety feels more like a constant, background worry and physical tension that just never goes away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have sudden episodes of intense fear or panic that peak within minutes, with symptoms like shortness of breath, chest pain, dizziness, or feeling like something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those sudden, intense panic attacks with shortness of breath or dizziness. My anxiety is much more of a constant, lingering worry and physical tension that just never seems to lift.\n\nDoctor: How much has this constant worry and tension been affecting your daily life, such as your work, relationships, or ability to relax?\n\nPatient: It has had a major impact; I've started making mistakes at work, like missing clauses in contracts and forgetting to return client calls on time. I've also been turning down social invitations because I feel too exhausted and on edge to be present with friends or family. It has gotten to the point where I find it nearly impossible to relax at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you started, stopped, or changed any medications, caffeine, alcohol, or other substances around the time this anxiety began, or do you have any thyroid or other medical problems?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't started or changed any medications, and I don't drink much caffeine or alcohol. I also don't have any medical conditions like thyroid issues that I'm aware of.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you find it very hard to control or stop the worry even when you know it may be excessive?\n\nPatient: Yes, it feels absolutely impossible to stop once it starts. Even when I tell myself, \"Parin, stop, this is ridiculous,\" the worries just chain one into another. If a deal falls through, I start spiraling about my commission, then my mortgage, and suddenly I'm picturing myself losing my home and having to move in with someone out of desperation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had brief, unexpected episodes of intense fear that peak within minutes, with symptoms like chest pain, shortness of breath, dizziness, or a feeling that something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced those sudden, brief episodes of intense fear or panic. My anxiety is much more of a constant, ongoing background worry and physical tension that stays with me throughout the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about a lot of different areas of your life, such as work, finances, health, family, and everyday responsibilities, most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, I worry about almost all of those areas on most days, and this constant cycle about my career, finances, my house, and my niece has been going on for over a year. My worries just chain together uncontrollably; a simple worry about a work deal quickly spirals into worrying about my commission, my mortgage, my health insurance, and eventually ending up old and alone. It feels like there is no area of my life that the worry doesn't touch.\n\nDoctor: Do you also get symptoms like muscle tension, restlessness, irritability, or trouble sleeping because of the worry?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I definitely struggle with both muscle tension and sleep issues. I lie awake until 1 or 2 AM with my mind spinning about clients and money, and I often wake up at 4 or 5 AM with my heart racing. Also, my shoulders and neck feel like concrete by the end of the day, and I clench my jaw so tightly during drives that it gives me headaches.

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
